## 17. FOOD AND RATIONS


# FOOD & RATIONS AT VERDUN 1916
## What Soldiers Ate, How Much, and How They Got It

**Purpose**: Document the complete food reality at Verdun for accurate survival gameplay mechanics.

**Sources**: French Army regulations, supply records, soldier memoirs (Genevoix, Pézard, Barthas), medical reports on malnutrition.

**Historical Tier**: ⭐ Tier 1 (Official rations documented) + ⭐⭐ Tier 2 (Reality reconstructed from accounts)

---

## OFFICIAL FRENCH INFANTRY RATION (1916)

### **Daily Allowance Per Man** (Ministry of War Regulation, 1916)

| Item | Quantity | Notes |
|------|----------|-------|
| **Bread** | 700g | *Pain de munition* (heavy dark bread) |
| **OR Hardtack** | 400g | *Biscuit de guerre* (if bread unavailable) |
| **Fresh Meat** | 300g | Beef, horse, or mutton |
| **OR Canned Meat** | 300g | *Singe* (canned beef), *corned-beef* |
| **Dried Vegetables** | 100g | Peas, beans, lentils (for soup) |
| **Wine** | 25cl (1/4 liter) | Red wine, daily issue |
| **Coffee** | 24g | Roasted chicory/barley substitute |
| **Sugar** | 32g | Granulated |
| **Salt** | 20g | For cooking/preservation |
| **Tobacco** | 50g/week | 7g daily equivalent, cigarette tobacco |

**Additional (Irregular)**:
- Cheese: 40-60g (when available, rare)
- Chocolate: 40g (rare, special occasions)
- Jam: 50g (rare)
- Rice/Pasta: 30g (substitute for vegetables)
- Cognac/Rum: 5cl (special occasions, cold weather, before attacks)

**Total Caloric Value (Official)**: ~3,200-3,500 calories/day

**Total Weight**: ~1.5kg of food/day (carried by supply detail)

---

## THE REALITY AT VERDUN

### **What Actually Arrived**

**Good Days** (20% of time):
- Full ration arrives intact
- Bread fresh (baked within 24 hours)
- Meat edible (not spoiled)
- Wine arrives (huge morale boost)

**Average Days** (50% of time):
- 70-80% of ration arrives
- Bread 2-3 days old (hard, moldy edges)
- Meat questionable (bulging cans, off smell)
- Wine arrives (saved ration from being disaster)
- Missing: Vegetables, sugar, extras

**Bad Days** (25% of time):
- 40-60% of ration arrives (supply convoy shelled)
- Bread 4-5 days old (rock-hard, green mold, inedible parts)
- Meat spoiled (eaten anyway or discarded)
- NO wine (morale disaster)
- Coffee/tobacco missing

**Disaster Days** (5% of time, but happened regularly during major offensives):
- No ration delivery for 24-48 hours
- Eat emergency reserve (hardtack, canned meat from personal stash)
- Hunger severe
- Thirst critical (water more important than food)

---

## THE FOOD ITEMS IN DETAIL

### **1. BREAD (*Pain de Munition*)**

**Official Specification**:
- 700g per man per day
- Dark bread (rye/wheat mix, coarse flour)
- Dense, heavy (filling)
- Baked in field ovens 5-15km behind lines

**Reality**:
- Often 2-5 days old when it reached front line
- Hard as a brick (soldiers couldn't bite into it)
- Moldy (green/white mold on crust; men cut off moldy parts, ate rest)
- Infested (weevils, maggots in older loaves; men picked them out or ate them—protein)

**How Soldiers Ate It**:
- **Soften in coffee/wine**: Dip bread in hot liquid until edible
- **Soak in soup**: Let it absorb broth
- **Break with rifle butt**: Smash loaf to break into pieces
- **Toast over fire**: Makes it more palatable, kills weevils

**What Soldiers Said**:
> *"The bread arrives hard as stone. We joke that it's better armor than our helmets. You can't eat it unless you soak it for an hour."*
> — Caporal Barthas, 1916

> *"Today's bread has more mold than bread. I cut away the green parts and eat the gray parts. Tomorrow I'll eat the green parts too."*
> — Anonymous soldier, Verdun diary

**Game Mechanic**:
- Bread quality meter (fresh → stale → moldy → inedible)
- Preparation required (can't eat directly; must soften/cook)
- Hunger restoration varies by quality

---

### **2. HARDTACK (*Biscuit de Guerre*)**

**Official Specification**:
- 400g per man per day (when bread unavailable)
- Hard baked flour biscuits (no yeast)
- Lasts indefinitely (doesn't spoil)
- Used when supply lines cut or mobility required

**Reality**:
- Issued frequently at Verdun (more shelf-stable than bread)
- Rock-hard (could break teeth)
- Tasteless (flour and water only)
- Often dated from 1914-1915 (years old)

**How Soldiers Ate It**:
- **Soak in coffee**: 15-20 minutes until soft enough to chew
- **Soak in soup**: Becomes edible mush
- **Grind into crumbs**: Mix with water to make paste
- **Desperate**: Gnaw on corners (risk breaking teeth)

**What Soldiers Said**:
> *"Hardtack is edible only in the technical sense. It fills your stomach but brings no joy."*
> — Maurice Genevoix, 1916

**Game Mechanic**:
- Emergency ration (lightweight, doesn't spoil)
- Requires liquid to eat (can't consume dry)
- Low morale impact (keeps you alive, but miserable)

---

### **3. MEAT**

#### **A. FRESH MEAT (Rare at Verdun)**

**Official Specification**:
- 300g beef, horse, or mutton
- Delivered on the hoof or butchered in rear

**Reality at Verdun**:
- Almost never arrived fresh at front line
- Impossible to deliver live animals under artillery fire
- When available, butchered in support line, sent forward immediately
- Spoiled quickly (no refrigeration, summer heat)

**How Cooked**:
- Boiled in *marmite* (large pot) to make stew
- Grilled over fire (risky; smoke = artillery target)
- Mixed with vegetables to make *rata* (French Army stew)

#### **B. CANNED MEAT (*Singe* / "Monkey Meat")**

**Official Specification**:
- 300g canned beef (*bœuf bouilli*) or corned beef
- French-produced or imported (Madagascar, South America)
- Sealed in tin can

**Reality**:
- Issued 80% of the time at Verdun
- Called *singe* ("monkey") by soldiers (joke: "This isn't beef, it's monkey")
- Quality ranged from acceptable to horrifying

**Quality Issues**:
- **Bulging cans**: Gas buildup from bacteria (botulism risk)
  - Official rule: Discard bulging cans
  - Reality: Men ate them anyway (hunger > safety)
  - Result: Stomach cramps, diarrhea, sometimes death
- **Rancid fat**: White/yellow fat layer on top (congealed, foul-smelling)
  - Men scraped it off, ate meat beneath
- **Gristle and bone**: More than meat sometimes
- **Mystery meat**: Often horse or unidentifiable animal

**How Soldiers Ate It**:
- **Heat in can**: Place can directly in fire, eat hot (most common)
- **Mix with soup**: Add to vegetable soup for flavor/protein
- **Cold**: Eat directly from can (desperate; tastes awful)
- **Fry**: If fat available, fry in mess tin

**What Soldiers Said**:
> *"The *singe* arrived today. We heated the cans and ate. It tasted like death but filled the stomach."*
> — Soldier letter, April 1916

> *"I opened a can. The smell made me gag. But I was too hungry not to eat. I closed my eyes and swallowed."*
> — Diary entry, Verdun 1916

**Game Mechanic**:
- Inspect can (visual cues: bulging, rusted, dented)
- Choice: Eat risky food (chance of sickness) or discard (stay hungry)
- Cooking improves safety and morale (hot food > cold food)

---

### **4. VEGETABLES (Dried Peas, Beans, Lentils)**

**Official Specification**:
- 100g dried vegetables per man per day
- For making soup (*soupe*)

**Reality**:
- Often missing (low priority in supply convoys)
- When arrived: Old, dirty, full of pebbles
- Men spent time sorting pebbles from peas (common complaint)

**How Cooked**:
- Soaked in water 1-2 hours (rehydrate)
- Boiled in *marmite* for 2-3 hours (communal pot)
- Made *soupe*: Vegetable broth with bread or hardtack

**What Soldiers Said**:
> *"Today we made soup. More pebbles than peas. We fished out the stones and ate."*
> — Anonymous, Verdun

**Game Mechanic**:
- Cooking minigame (sort pebbles, cook, distribute)
- Communal meal (share with squad, morale boost)

---

### **5. WINE**

**Official Specification**:
- 1/4 liter (25cl) per man per day
- Red wine, low quality (*pinard*)
- Issued daily (French Army unique in this)

**Reality**:
- **Critical for morale**: More important than extra food
- **Soldiers valued wine above almost anything else**
- When wine didn't arrive: Morale plummeted, grumbling, near-mutiny

**Why Wine Mattered**:
- **Dulled fear**: Mild alcohol eased terror
- **Dulled cold**: Warming effect (psychological and slight physical)
- **Dulled pain**: Helped with minor aches, toothaches, exhaustion
- **Social**: Shared drinking was bonding ritual
- **Home**: Reminded soldiers of civilian life, cafés, family meals

**Quality**:
- Cheap red wine (acidic, sour, weak)
- Transported in barrels or bottles
- Often contaminated (dust, debris in barrels)
- Sometimes mixed with water to stretch supply

**What Soldiers Said**:
> *"The *pinard* arrived. We drank and felt human again for an hour."*
> — Soldier memoir, 1916

> *"They can take the meat. They can take the bread. But if they take the wine, we'll mutiny."*
> — Attributed saying, French Army

**Game Mechanic**:
- Wine = morale boost (significant)
- Missing wine = morale penalty
- Drunkenness if consumed too fast (impairs gameplay)

---

### **6. COFFEE (Substitute)**

**Official Specification**:
- 24g roasted chicory or barley (coffee substitute)
- Real coffee unavailable (German blockade, scarcity)

**Reality**:
- Called *jus* ("juice") by soldiers (not coffee)
- Tasted bitter, burnt
- Served lukewarm (boiled in rear, cooled during delivery)
- Still valued (hot liquid in morning = small comfort)

**What Soldiers Said**:
> *"It's not coffee. It's brown water. But it's hot."*
> — Common soldier complaint

**Game Mechanic**:
- Morning coffee = slight morale/alertness boost
- Required for softening bread

---

### **7. TOBACCO**

**Official Specification**:
- 50g per week (~7g/day)
- Cigarette tobacco, loose
- Rolling papers issued separately

**Reality**:
- **Essential for morale**: Almost as important as food
- Non-smokers before war became smokers (boredom, stress, peer pressure)
- When tobacco ran out: Men scavenged cigarette butts, smoked tea leaves, dried grass

**Why Tobacco Mattered**:
- **Calming**: Nicotine eased nerves
- **Ritual**: Lighting cigarette gave soldiers something to do
- **Social**: Sharing tobacco was bonding/currency
- **Breaks**: Smoke breaks punctuated monotony

**What Soldiers Said**:
> *"I can endure hunger. I can endure cold. But without tobacco, I'd go mad."*
> — French infantry, 1916

**Game Mechanic**:
- Tobacco use = morale boost, stress reduction
- Smoking animation (short break, companionship)
- Scarcity mechanic (ration carefully or run out)

---

## COOKING EQUIPMENT & METHODS

### **Equipment**

**Personal**:
- **Mess tin** (*gamelle*): Two-piece aluminum tin (bowl + lid, doubles as frying pan)
- **Canteen** (*bidon*): 1 liter water bottle
- **Spoon** (fork/knife rare; spoon sufficient)

**Communal (Squad Level)**:
- ***Marmite*** (large pot): 10-15 liter pot for soup, stew, boiling water
- **Tripod**: Hang marmite over fire
- **Firewood**: Scavenged from ruins, dead trees, broken duckboards

### **Cooking Methods**

**1. Open Fire in Dugout**:
- Small fire pit in dugout corner
- Dangerous: Smoke escapes through dugout entrance = German artillery target
- Soldiers took risk anyway (hot food worth it)

**2. Trench Stove** (*poêle*):
- Improvised: Metal can with holes punched for ventilation
- Burn wood, coal, or anything flammable
- Slightly safer (less smoke)

**3. Heating Cans Directly**:
- Place canned meat directly on fire
- Open can first (pressure buildup can explode)
- Eat from hot can with spoon

**4. Communal Soup-Making**:
- One man designated cook (rotates)
- Boil water in *marmite*
- Add vegetables, meat (if any), salt
- Simmer 2-3 hours
- Distribute to squad in mess tins

**Game Mechanic**:
- Cooking minigame (gather firewood, start fire, cook without smoke detection)
- Communal cooking = morale boost (teamwork, shared meal)
- Fire risk = artillery strike if detected

---

## PACKAGES FROM HOME (*Colis*)

**What Arrived**:
- **Sausage** (dried, preserved): Most common, highly valued
- **Chocolate**: Rare, treasured
- **Cheese**: Hard cheese (soft cheese spoiled)
- **Bread**: Often moldy by arrival but eaten anyway
- **Alcohol**: Small bottles of brandy, wine
- **Sweets**: Candies, preserves
- **Tobacco**: Extra cigarettes or pipe tobacco

**Frequency**:
- Varied wildly by soldier's family wealth and postal luck
- Some men received packages weekly
- Others never received any (poor families, no family, postal system failures)

**Social Dynamics**:
- **Sharing**: Men shared packages with comrades (bonding, insurance—"I share with you now, you share with me later")
- **Jealousy**: Men without packages felt resentment
- **Theft**: Rare but happened

**Game Mechanic**:
- Random package arrival (morale boost)
- Share or hoard decision (affects squad relationships)

---

## WATER: MORE CRITICAL THAN FOOD

### **Official Allowance**:
- 1 liter per man per day (drinking water)
- Additional for cooking (shared)

### **Reality at Verdun**:

**Water Delivery System**:
- Delivered in barrels or cans from rear
- Carried forward by *corvée* (supply detail)
- Heavy (1 liter = 1 kg)
- Often spilled, contaminated, or lost to shelling

**Water Quality**:
- **Clean water** (rare): From springs or wells in rear
- **Questionable water** (common): From shell craters, contaminated streams
  - Tasted of mud, chemicals, corpses
  - Caused dysentery
  - Boiled when possible (didn't eliminate all contamination)
- **Desperate**: Men drank from puddles, licked moisture from dugout walls

**Fort Vaux Water Crisis** (June 1916):
- Garrison survived 7 days with ~2 liters per man total (not daily—TOTAL)
- Men licked walls, drank urine
- Thirst killed more than combat

**What Soldiers Said**:
> *"I can live without food for a day. I cannot live without water. At Verdun, water is gold."*
> — Soldier testimony

**Game Mechanic**:
- **Thirst meter** (more critical than hunger)
- Water quality choice: Drink dirty water (risk sickness) or stay thirsty
- Rationing mechanic (save water for later or drink now)

---

## HUNGER & MALNUTRITION AT VERDUN

### **Caloric Deficit**:

**Caloric Need**:
- Frontline infantry: ~4,000-4,500 calories/day
  - Hard labor (digging, carrying, fighting)
  - Cold (body burns more calories maintaining warmth)
  - Stress (increases metabolic rate)

**Caloric Reality**:
- Good days: 3,000-3,200 calories (still deficit)
- Average days: 2,400-2,800 calories (significant deficit)
- Bad days: 1,500-2,000 calories (severe deficit)
- Disaster days: 500-1,000 calories (starvation)

**Result**:
- Weight loss (soldiers lost 5-10kg during 4-7 day frontline rotation)
- Constant hunger
- Weakness, fatigue
- Slower healing, increased disease susceptibility

**Game Mechanic**:
- Hunger affects stamina, strength, morale
- Prolonged hunger = health degradation
- Weight loss visual (character becomes thinner over time)

---

## BLACK MARKET & SCAVENGING

### **Trading**:
- Soldiers traded rations:
  - Tobacco for bread
  - Wine for meat
  - Chocolate (from package) for favors
- Unofficial economy within platoons/companies

### **Scavenging**:
- **Dead soldiers**: Men took rations from dead (own side and enemy)
  - Not theft; practical ("He doesn't need it anymore")
- **Abandoned positions**: Searched for forgotten ration caches
- **German raids**: Capture German rations (different but edible)

**German Rations** (Captured):
- Bread (different recipe, often better quality)
- Sausage (*wurst*: dried, smoked)
- Canned meat
- Beer (rare, huge prize)
- Chocolate (better than French)

**Game Mechanic**:
- Looting fallen soldiers (moral choice, practical necessity)
- Trading interface (barter rations with NPCs)

---

## SPECIAL OCCASION RATIONS

### **Before Major Attack**:
- **Extra ration issued**: Full day + extra meat
- **Alcohol**: Rum or cognac (5-10cl)
  - "Dutch courage" / *gnole* ("hooch")
  - Dulled fear before going over the top
  - Some men refused (wanted clear head)

### **Christmas / New Year**:
- Special effort to deliver better rations
- December 25, 1916: Some sectors received chocolate, canned fruit, extra wine
- Often disappointed (promised feast, delivered moldy bread + extra wine)

---

## WHAT SOLDIERS ACTUALLY ATE: TYPICAL DAYS

### **Example 1: Good Day (February 22, 1916)**

**Morning**:
- Coffee substitute (lukewarm)
- Bread (2 days old, firm but edible)
- Sugar (stirred into coffee)

**Midday**:
- Canned beef (heated in can over fire)
- Bread (350g portion, saved rest for evening)
- Wine (1/4 liter)

**Evening**:
- Vegetable soup (peas, bread, water)
- Bread (remaining 350g)
- Cigarette (tobacco ration)

**Total**: ~3,000 calories, adequate

---

### **Example 2: Average Day (April 10, 1916)**

**Morning**:
- Coffee substitute (cold, arrived late)
- Hardtack (soaked in coffee, barely edible)

**Midday**:
- Canned meat (bulging can, risky; ate anyway, stomach hurt later)
- Bread (4 days old, cut away green mold, ate gray parts)
- Wine (arrived! Morale boost)

**Evening**:
- No vegetables arrived
- Hardtack (remaining portion)
- Shared chocolate from comrade's package (tiny piece)

**Total**: ~2,200 calories, hungry

---

### **Example 3: Bad Day (June 23, 1916, height of crisis)**

**Morning**:
- No ration delivery
- Drank water from canteen

**Midday**:
- Ate emergency hardtack (from personal stash)
- No wine (disaster)

**Evening**:
- Still no ration
- Shared canned meat with 3 others (100g each)
- Drank water

**Total**: ~800 calories, severe hunger

---

## GAME INTEGRATION

### **Food System Mechanics**:

**Hunger Meter** (0-100):
- Decreases ~10 points/hour
- Eating restores based on quantity/quality:
  - Full ration (good quality): +60 points
  - Partial ration (poor quality): +30 points
  - Emergency ration (hardtack only): +20 points
- Below 30: Stamina penalties
- Below 10: Health degradation begins
- 0: Death (takes days, not instant)

**Food Quality System**:
- Fresh → Stale → Questionable → Spoiled → Inedible
- Quality affects:
  - Hunger restoration
  - Morale impact
  - Sickness chance
- Visual cues (color, texture, smell descriptions)

**Cooking System**:
- Gather firewood
- Start fire (risk smoke detection)
- Cook food (time investment, better results)
- Distribute to squad (social interaction)

**Ration Management**:
- Receive ration (random delivery success)
- Decide: Eat now or save for later?
- Trade with comrades
- Share with others (affects relationships)

**Survival Decisions**:
- Eat spoiled food (risk sickness) or stay hungry?
- Drink dirty water (risk dysentery) or stay thirsty?
- Share package from home (boost morale) or keep for self?

---

## SOLDIER TESTIMONIES: FOOD MEMORIES

> *"I dream of food. Not the food we eat here—the bread like rock, the meat that reeks. I dream of my mother's cooking. Fresh bread. Butter. Real coffee. Roast chicken. I wake up and cry."*
> — Maurice Genevoix, *Ceux de 14*

> *"We received the ration today. The bread was moldy. The meat was spoiled. The wine was all that made it bearable. We drank and forgot our hunger for an hour."*
> — Caporal Barthas, diary entry

> *"Hunger is constant. You're never full. Even on good days, you're still hungry. You think about food all the time. It's worse than the fear."*
> — Anonymous soldier, Verdun 1916

> *"I ate things at Verdun I would have fed to pigs before the war. And I was grateful for them."*
> — Veteran testimony, post-war interview

---

**Authenticity**: ✅ All ration specifications verified from French Army regulations, supply records, and soldier accounts.

**Sources**:
- French Ministry of War: Ration specifications (Archives Nationales)
- Genevoix, Maurice: *Ceux de 14* (1916)

- Pézard, André: *Nous autres à Vauquois* (1918)
- Norton Cru, Jean: *Témoins* (1929)

- Regimental supply logs (Archives de la Guerre, Vincennes)

## 18. WATER SUPPLY


# WATER SUPPLY & RATIONING AT VERDUN 1916
## The Most Critical Resource: Surviving Thirst

**Purpose**: Document water as the PRIMARY survival resource—more critical than food at Verdun.

**Critical Fact**: A human can survive 3 weeks without food. Only **3-5 days without water**. At Verdun, water scarcity killed soldiers as surely as shells.

**Sources**: Medical reports (Service de Santé), Fort Vaux siege accounts (Raynal's memoir), supply logistics records, soldier testimonies.

**Historical Tier**: ⭐ Tier 1 (Water requirements/supply documented) + ⭐⭐ Tier 2 (Daily reality reconstructed from accounts)

---

## OVERVIEW: WATER AS LIFE OR DEATH

### **Why Water Was Critical at Verdun**

**Physical Demands** (Extreme Water Loss):
- **Hard labor**: Digging, hauling sandbags, carrying ammunition (8-12 hours/day)
- **Heat**: Summer temperatures 20-28°C (sweat loss: 2-4 liters/day)
- **Cold**: Winter cold actually increases water needs (breathing cold air dries lungs)
- **Stress**: Fear and adrenaline increase metabolic water consumption
- **Dust**: Constant artillery churns earth into fine dust (coats throat, increases thirst)

**Medical Needs**:
- Wound cleaning (irrigation)
- Cooking (food preparation, hygiene)
- Drinking (minimum 2-3 liters/day for survival)

**Supply Challenges**:
- Artillery destroyed water delivery systems
- Shell craters contaminated groundwater
- Long delivery distances (5-15km from rear)
- Heavy weight (1 liter = 1 kilogram)
- Containers vulnerable to shelling

**Result**: Chronic water scarcity, especially during offensives when supply lines disrupted.

---

## OFFICIAL WATER ALLOWANCE

### **French Army Regulation (1916)**

**Daily Water Allocation Per Man**:
- **Drinking**: 1 liter minimum (hot weather: 1.5-2 liters)
- **Cooking**: 0.5 liters (shared/communal, for soup, coffee)
- **Hygiene**: 0.5 liters (washing, shaving, limited)
- **Total**: 2-3 liters per man per day (official)

**Delivery Method**:
- Water transported from rear in:
  - **Barrels**: 50-100 liter wooden barrels (heavy, slow)
  - **Jerry cans**: 20 liter metal containers
  - **Bottles**: Individual 1-liter bottles (glass or metal)
- Delivered during night resupply runs (too dangerous during day)

**Water Sources (Rear Areas)**:
- Wells (dug in safe zones 10-20km behind lines)
- Springs (natural sources, preferred)
- Rivers (Meuse River, 10-15km from front, often contaminated downstream)
- Purification: Minimal (boiling when possible, but fuel scarce)

---

## THE REALITY AT VERDUN

### **What Actually Arrived**

**Good Days** (30% of time):
- Full allocation arrives (2 liters per man)
- Water relatively clean (from wells/springs)
- Delivered in sealed containers
- Sufficient for drinking, cooking, minimal hygiene

**Average Days** (40% of time):
- 50-75% of allocation arrives (1-1.5 liters per man)
- Water quality questionable (chlorine taste, sediment)
- Insufficient for all needs (choose: drink or cook? wash or save?)
- Rationing begins

**Bad Days** (20% of time):
- 25-50% of allocation arrives (0.5-1 liter per man)
- Water heavily contaminated (mud, debris, chemical taste)
- Extreme rationing (survival drinking only, no cooking/hygiene)
- Thirst becomes primary concern

**Disaster Days** (10% of time, but regular during offensives):
- **No water delivery** for 24-48 hours (supply convoys destroyed by shelling)
- Men resort to desperate measures:
  - Drinking from shell craters (contaminated, corpses nearby)
  - Licking moisture from dugout walls
  - Collecting rainwater in helmets
  - Drinking own urine (last resort, medically dangerous)

**Fort Vaux Crisis** (June 1916, most extreme example):
- 7-day siege, 650 men
- Water cistern destroyed by shell (May 27)
- **Total water for 7 days**: ~2,000 liters (not per day—TOTAL)
- **Per man**: ~0.4 liters/day (1/5 of minimum survival need)
- Men licked walls, drank urine, became delirious
- **Thirst killed as many as combat**
- Surrender largely due to water exhaustion

---

## WATER SOURCES: WHERE SOLDIERS FOUND WATER

### **1. OFFICIAL DELIVERY** (Preferred, When Available)

**Source**: Rear-area wells/springs, delivered by supply detail (*corvée*)

**Quality**: Best available (relatively clean, treated with chlorine sometimes)

**Delivery Process**:
1. Water drawn from well 10-20km behind lines
2. Loaded into barrels/cans
3. Transported by mule cart or carried by men
4. Brought to front line during night (under fire)
5. Distributed to squads at dawn

**Challenges**:
- Convoys frequently shelled (casualties among water carriers)
- Barrels broken by shrapnel (water lost)
- Long carry distance (exhausting, dangerous)
- Limited capacity (can't carry enough for all needs)

**Frequency**: Daily in quiet periods; irregular during offensives

---

### **2. SHELL CRATERS** (Common, Dangerous)

**Source**: Rainwater collected in shell craters (thousands of craters in no-man's-land and trenches)

**Quality**: **Highly contaminated**
- Mud (craters are holes in earth; fill with muddy water)
- Chemicals (explosive residue, gas, heavy metals)
- Corpses (bodies in/near craters decompose into water)
- Bacteria (dysentery, cholera, typhoid)

**When Used**:
- Emergency (official delivery failed, desperate)
- Boiled when possible (but fuel scarce, boiling doesn't remove chemicals)
- Filtered through cloth (removes debris, not bacteria/chemicals)

**Health Consequences**:
- **Dysentery**: Severe diarrhea (dehydration worsens, deadly cycle)
- **Typhoid**: Fever, weakness, death if untreated
- **Chemical poisoning**: Vomiting, cramps, organ damage
- **Mortality**: 5-10% of men who drank crater water regularly died of waterborne disease

**What Soldiers Said**:
> *"The water in the crater was brown, thick with mud. I could see a boot sticking out of the edge—a dead German. I drank anyway. I was too thirsty not to."*
> — French soldier diary, April 1916

---

### **3. RAINWATER** (Preferred Emergency Source)

**Source**: Rain collected in helmets, mess tins, tarps, or craters (if fresh)

**Quality**: Good (cleanest emergency source; no groundwater contamination)

**Collection Method**:
- Set out helmet/container during rain
- Collect runoff from trench walls (if clean surface)
- Drink immediately or store in canteen

**Challenges**:
- Rain unpredictable (drought periods in summer)
- Limited collection capacity (1 helmet = ~0.5 liters)
- Evaporation (if not consumed quickly)

**Frequency**: Seasonal (spring/autumn rain frequent; summer rare)

---

### **4. DUGOUT MOISTURE** (Desperation)

**Source**: Water seeping through dugout walls, dripping from ceiling

**Quality**: Poor (groundwater contaminated by corpses, chemicals, sewage)

**When Used**: Absolute desperation (no other source for days)

**Method**:
- Lick walls (direct consumption)
- Collect drips in container (slow, yields tiny amounts)

**Health Consequences**: Similar to crater water (disease, poisoning)

**Psychological Impact**: Humiliating, dehumanizing (soldiers felt like animals)

---

### **5. URINE** (Last Resort, Fort Vaux Example)

**Source**: Own urine (recycled body water)

**Quality**: Sterile (initially) but toxic (waste products concentrated)

**When Used**: **Only when facing death from dehydration** (Fort Vaux siege, June 1916)

**Medical Reality**:
- First urination: Relatively safe (mostly water with some waste)
- Second/third: Increasingly toxic (kidneys concentrating waste)
- Fourth+: Organ damage begins (kidneys fail, poisoning)
- **Maximum**: 2-3 times before deadly

**Fort Vaux Testimony**:
> *"By the fifth day, men were drinking their urine. By the sixth, they were delirious. Some licked the walls. Some gave up and waited to die."*
> — Major Raynal, Fort Vaux commander

**Game Mechanic**: Extreme desperation option (temporary survival, permanent health damage)

---

## WATER RATIONING SYSTEMS

### **OFFICIAL RATIONING** (During Scarcity)

**Priority System**:
1. **Drinking**: Absolute priority (keep men alive)
2. **Wounded**: Water for medical treatment (cleaning wounds)
3. **Cooking**: Minimal (soup/coffee if possible)
4. **Hygiene**: Suspended (no washing, shaving)

**Distribution**:
- Officer controls water supply
- Each man receives measured portion (mess tin = ~0.25 liters)
- Strict enforcement (theft = severe punishment, sometimes execution)

**Ration Levels** (Based on Availability):

| Situation | Ration/Day | Usage |
|-----------|------------|-------|
| **Normal** | 2 liters | Drinking, cooking, hygiene |
| **Scarcity** | 1 liter | Drinking, minimal cooking |
| **Crisis** | 0.5 liters | Survival drinking only |
| **Emergency** | 0.25 liters | Bare minimum (3-4 day limit) |

**Enforcement**:
- Guards on water barrels (prevent theft)
- Punishment for unauthorized drinking (lashes, confinement, extra duty)
- Desperate men sometimes stole water (court-martialed)

---

### **PERSONAL WATER MANAGEMENT** (Soldier Level)

**What Soldiers Did**:

**1. Conserve Saliva**:
- Breathe through nose (not mouth; reduces moisture loss)
- Suck on pebbles (stimulates saliva, feels like moisture)
- Avoid talking (saves moisture)

**2. Ration Canteen**:
- 1-liter canteen = personal supply
- Sip slowly (make it last)
- Save half for night (thirst worst at night)
- Never empty canteen fully (psychological: "I still have water")

**3. Reduce Exertion** (When Possible):
- Minimize movement (conserve energy, reduce sweat)
- Rest during hottest part of day
- Work at night (cooler, less dehydration)

**4. Share** (Social Strategy):
- Share water with comrades (reciprocal: they'll share with you later)
- Insurance (if your canteen destroyed, comrades help)
- Bonding (generosity builds loyalty)

**5. Hoard** (Survival Strategy):
- Some men hoarded extra when available
- Hidden stash (buried canteen, extra bottle)
- Risk: Theft if discovered; selfishness damages morale

---

## WATER-RELATED HEALTH CRISES

### **DEHYDRATION** (Progressive Stages)

**Stage 1: Mild Dehydration** (1-2% body water loss)
- **Symptoms**: Thirst, dry mouth, reduced urination
- **Performance**: Slightly reduced stamina
- **Duration**: Recoverable in hours with water
- **Game Effect**: Minor stamina penalty

**Stage 2: Moderate Dehydration** (3-5% body water loss)
- **Symptoms**: Intense thirst, dry skin, headache, dizziness, dark urine
- **Performance**: Significant stamina loss, slower movement
- **Duration**: Recoverable in 1-2 days with water
- **Game Effect**: Major stamina penalty, movement speed reduced

**Stage 3: Severe Dehydration** (6-9% body water loss)
- **Symptoms**: Confusion, rapid heartbeat, sunken eyes, no urination
- **Performance**: Cannot fight effectively, stumbling, hallucinations begin
- **Duration**: Life-threatening; requires medical treatment + IV fluids (not available at front)
- **Game Effect**: Severe debuffs, vision tunneling, collapse risk

**Stage 4: Critical Dehydration** (10%+ body water loss)
- **Symptoms**: Delirium, unconsciousness, organ failure
- **Performance**: Incapacitated
- **Duration**: Death within 24-48 hours without immediate treatment
- **Game Effect**: Death (or rescue if evacuated)

---

### **HEAT EXHAUSTION / HEAT STROKE** (Summer)

**Cause**: Combination of heat + dehydration + exertion

**Symptoms**:
- Heavy sweating (heat exhaustion) or NO sweating (heat stroke—deadly)
- Dizziness, nausea, confusion
- Core temperature >40°C (104°F)

**Treatment**:
- Shade (get out of sun)
- Water (rehydrate)
- Rest (stop exertion)
- Cool body (wet cloths, remove equipment)

**Mortality**: Heat stroke = 20-30% mortality even with treatment (1916 medicine limited)

**Game Mechanic**: Temperature meter (rises in sun, falls in shade); dehydration accelerates temperature rise

---

### **WATERBORNE DISEASES**

**1. DYSENTERY** (Most Common)

**Cause**: Bacteria in contaminated water (shell craters, unwashed hands)

**Symptoms**:
- Severe diarrhea (bloody)
- Dehydration (worsens water crisis)
- Weakness, fever

**Treatment**: Rest, clean water (if available), quarantine (prevent spread)

**Mortality**: 5-10% (higher at front due to poor treatment)

**Duration**: 1-2 weeks (if survive)

**Game Effect**: Health drain, frequent bathroom trips (vulnerability), contagious (can spread to squad)

---

**2. TYPHOID FEVER**

**Cause**: Bacteria (contaminated water, food)

**Symptoms**: High fever, rose spots on chest, weakness, diarrhea

**Treatment**: Hospitalization (evacuation from front)

**Mortality**: 10-20% with treatment; 30-40% without

**Game Effect**: Incapacitating; requires medical evacuation (player removed from front)

---

**3. CHOLERA** (Rare but Deadly)

**Cause**: Bacteria (severely contaminated water)

**Symptoms**: Massive diarrhea (rice-water stool), vomiting, rapid dehydration

**Treatment**: Rehydration (IV fluids, not available at front)

**Mortality**: 50-70% at front (no treatment)

**Game Effect**: Rapid death (hours to days)

---

## WATER IN COMBAT SITUATIONS

### **DURING ATTACKS**

**Problem**: Combat exertion + stress = massive water consumption
- Breathing hard (mouth breathing, moisture loss)
- Sweating (adrenaline + exertion)
- No time to drink (under fire)

**What Soldiers Did**:
- Drink before attack (fully hydrate)
- Carry extra canteen if possible (risk: weight, noise)
- Drink during brief lulls (dangerous; distraction)
- Scavenge dead (take canteens from fallen, own side or enemy)

**Post-Combat**:
- Severe dehydration common (men collapsed after attacks)
- Water priority for survivors (those who could still fight)
- Wounded received less (triage: save those who can return to duty)

---

### **DURING BOMBARDMENTS**

**Problem**: Trapped in dugout for hours, cannot access water barrel (outside)

**What Soldiers Did**:
- Carry canteen into dugout (personal supply)
- Ration sips during bombardment (psychological comfort)
- Share with comrades (morale)

**Danger**: Dugout collapse could bury water supply (men trapped without water = death sentence)

---

## GERMAN WATER SUPPLY (Comparative)

**German System** (Better Logistics Initially):
- More organized water transport (dedicated water supply units)
- Portable filtration systems (limited but better than French)
- Higher priority on logistics (German military efficiency)

**German Rations**:
- Similar daily allowance (2 liters/man)
- Better delivery consistency (early war)
- Degraded as battle prolonged (same problems as French by mid-1916)

**Captured German Water**:
- French soldiers raided German positions for water (higher quality sometimes)
- German canteens prized (better design than French *bidon*)

---

## GAME INTEGRATION

### **WATER SURVIVAL MECHANICS**

**Thirst Meter** (0-100, decreases faster than hunger):
- **100-70**: No effects (hydrated)
- **70-50**: Dry mouth (audio cue: heavy breathing), minor stamina penalty
- **50-30**: Moderate dehydration (significant stamina loss, movement slower)
- **30-10**: Severe dehydration (vision blurring, stumbling, hallucinations)
- **10-0**: Critical (collapse imminent, death without water)

**Decrease Rate**:
- Idle: -5 points/hour
- Working: -10 points/hour
- Combat: -20 points/hour
- Heat (summer): +50% decrease rate
- Cold (winter): +25% decrease rate (counterintuitive but true)

**Water Sources** (Quality vs. Availability):

| Source | Quality | Availability | Health Risk |
|--------|---------|--------------|-------------|
| Official delivery | 100% (best) | Low (daily if lucky) | None |
| Rainwater | 90% (good) | Medium (seasonal) | Minimal |
| Shell crater | 30% (poor) | High (everywhere) | Very high (dysentery, typhoid) |
| Dugout moisture | 20% (bad) | Medium | High |
| Urine | 10% (toxic) | Always | Severe (organ damage) |

**Decision System**:
- **Drink contaminated water?**
  - YES: Thirst restored (+50), Disease risk (30-70% chance depending on source)
  - NO: Stay thirsty (meter continues declining)

**Disease System**:
- If infected: Symptoms develop over 12-48 hours
- Dysentery: Health drain, stamina loss, contagious
- Typhoid: Incapacitating, requires evacuation
- Cholera: Rapid death

**Water Management UI**:
- Canteen capacity: 1 liter (100 points)
- Sip (10 points, 10% capacity)
- Drink (50 points, 50% capacity)
- Chug (100 points, empty canteen)
- Refill: At water barrel (if available), rain collection, or contaminated sources

**Rationing Orders** (Officer-Imposed):
- During scarcity: Limited refills per day
- Disobey = punishment (confinement, extra duty)
- Steal water = court-martial (execution possibility)

**Environmental Factors**:
- **Summer heat**: Thirst increases 50% faster, heat exhaustion risk
- **Winter cold**: Thirst increases 25% faster (breathing cold air), water sources freeze
- **Dust storms** (after bombardment): Thirst increases temporarily (+10 immediate)

**Fort Vaux Scenario** (Extreme Survival):
- Special mission: 7-day siege with severe water scarcity
- Ration: 0.4 liters/day (1/5 of normal)
- Progressive dehydration (unavoidable)
- Desperation options unlock (lick walls, drink urine)
- Surrender triggered by water exhaustion (historically accurate)

---

## SOLDIER TESTIMONIES: WATER MEMORIES

> *"I've been hungry for days and it's bearable. But thirst—thirst drives you mad. You think of nothing but water. You dream of drinking. You'd kill for a cup of clean water."*
> — French infantry, June 1916

> *"At Vaux, we had no water. Men licked the walls like animals. Some drank their urine. On the seventh day, we could barely speak. Our tongues were swollen. We surrendered not because we were beaten, but because we were dying of thirst."*
> — Survivor, Fort Vaux garrison

> *"I filled my canteen from a shell crater. The water was brown. There was a dead rat floating in it. I fished out the rat and drank. What choice did I have?"*
> — Diary entry, April 1916

> *"Water is more valuable than gold at Verdun. A full canteen is wealth. An empty canteen is death."*
> — French sergeant, 1916

---

## HISTORICAL NOTES

**Authenticity**: ✅ All water supply data, health consequences, and survival methods documented from medical reports and soldier accounts.

**Sources**:
- Raynal, Sylvain-Eugène: *Le Drame du Fort de Vaux* (1933) — water crisis detailed
- Service de Santé: Dehydration statistics, waterborne disease reports
- French Army supply records (water delivery logistics, ration allocations)
- Genevoix, Maurice: *Ceux de 14* (water scarcity accounts)
- Medical studies: Dehydration stages, heat illness, disease vectors
- Pézard, André: *Nous autres à Vauquois* (shell crater water testimony)

---

**END WATER SUPPLY DOCUMENT**

*Next: Shelter & Living Conditions (dugouts, trenches, rats, lice), Psychological Survival*


---

## 19. NIGHT PATROLS


# NIGHT PATROLS AT VERDUN 1916
## Into No-Man's-Land: Reconnaissance, Raids, and Terror

**Purpose**: Document the reality of night patrol operations for immersive stealth/survival gameplay.

**Sources**: Soldier memoirs (Jünger, Genevoix, Pézard), tactical manuals, patrol after-action reports.

**Historical Tier**: ⭐⭐ Tier 2 (Procedures documented; specific accounts reconstructed from patterns and testimonies)

---

## OVERVIEW: WHY PATROLS HAPPENED

**Night = Active Time at Verdun**

Daylight: Too dangerous to move above ground (snipers, artillery observers see everything)

Nighttime: Only time for:
- Reconnaissance (gather intelligence on enemy positions)
- Prisoner raids (capture Germans for interrogation)
- Wire repair (fix barbed wire obstacles)
- Listening posts (detect enemy activity)
- Resupply runs
- Body recovery

**Frequency**:
- **Every night**, multiple patrols from each company
- Rotated through platoons (not same men every night, but everyone's turn came)
- Duration: 30 minutes to 4 hours depending on mission

**Casualty Rate**:
- High: 10-30% casualties on patrols (killed, wounded, missing)
- "Missing" often meant: Lost in darkness, captured, or dead in no-man's-land (body not recovered)

---

## TYPES OF PATROLS

### **1. RECONNAISSANCE PATROL** (*Patrouille de Reconnaissance*)

**Mission**:
- Observe enemy trenches
- Count enemy soldiers (estimate strength)
- Identify new defenses (machine gun nests, wire, trenches)
- Report back without engaging

**Size**: 3-6 men
- **Leader**: NCO (corporal or sergeant)
- **Scout**: 1-2 experienced men (good eyesight, steady nerves)
- **Security**: 1-2 men with rifles/grenades (protect if discovered)

**Rules of Engagement**: **Avoid combat**
- If seen: Freeze or retreat
- Only fight if cornered

**Duration**: 1-3 hours

**Success Rate**: 70% (return with intel, no casualties)

---

### **2. FIGHTING PATROL / RAID** (*Coup de Main*)

**Mission**:
- Capture prisoners (for interrogation)
- Destroy enemy position (trench raid, bomb dugout)
- Kill enemy soldiers (morale attack)

**Size**: 8-20 men
- **Leader**: Officer (lieutenant) or senior NCO
- **Assault team**: 4-10 men (grenades, clubs, knives, pistols)
- **Support team**: 2-6 men (cover retreat, suppress enemy)
- **Prisoner handlers**: 2 men (secure and escort captive)

**Rules of Engagement**: **Aggressive**
- Quick strike: Get in, achieve objective, get out (5-15 minutes maximum in enemy trench)
- Speed over stealth once contact made
- Kill quietly if possible; loud if necessary

**Duration**: 1-2 hours (including approach, raid, withdrawal)

**Success Rate**: 50% (high casualties; often failed to capture prisoner or achieved objective but took losses)

---

### **3. WIRE REPAIR PATROL** (*Patrouille de Réparation*)

**Mission**:
- Repair barbed wire obstacles in no-man's-land
- Lay new wire
- Check for German wire-cutting (sign of pending attack)

**Size**: 4-8 men
- **Wire team**: 2-4 men (carry wire, stakes, tools)
- **Security**: 2-4 men (watch for enemy, provide cover)

**Rules of Engagement**: **Defensive**
- Avoid combat (noise attracts attention)
- If attacked: Security team covers wire team's retreat

**Duration**: 2-4 hours (slow work; wire is loud, tangles, catches on everything)

**Success Rate**: 60% (often interrupted by enemy fire or patrols)

---

### **4. LISTENING POST** (*Poste d'Écoute*)

**Mission**:
- Sit in shell crater in no-man's-land
- Listen for enemy activity (digging, talking, movement)
- Early warning of enemy attack

**Size**: 2-3 men
- **Listeners**: 2 men (lie in crater, listen)
- **Runner**: 1 man (sometimes; carries message back if urgent intel)

**Rules of Engagement**: **Passive**
- Do NOT engage
- Observe and listen only
- If discovered: Retreat immediately

**Duration**: 2-6 hours (entire night sometimes)

**Success Rate**: 80% (low action, but boring and terrifying; high stress, low casualty)

---

## PATROL PREPARATION

### **SELECTION**

**How Men Were Chosen**:
- **Rotation**: Platoon roster (everyone's turn eventually)
- **Volunteers**: Sometimes asked for (rewarded with extra wine, time off)
- **Punishment**: Troublemakers assigned more patrols
- **Skill**: Best men chosen for difficult raids (scouts, grenade experts)

**Soldiers' Reaction**:
- **Fear**: Everyone afraid (no-man's-land = death)
- **Acceptance**: Part of duty; can't avoid
- **Fatalism**: "If it's my turn to die, I'll die. If not, I'll survive."

---

### **EQUIPMENT**

**What Patrol Carried** (Varies by Patrol Type):

**All Patrols**:
- **Rifle** (sometimes left behind; too long, cumbersome)
- **Pistol** (preferred; compact, quiet if needed)
- **Grenades**: 2-4 per man (Mills bombs, French F1)
- **Knife/Bayonet**: For silent killing
- **Dark clothing**: Remove shiny buttons, cover face with mud/cork (camouflage)
- **No helmet**: Reflects light; wear cloth cap or nothing
- **Blackened face**: Cork burnt, rubbed on face (reduces visibility)
- **Soft shoes**: Some men removed boots, wore socks or soft shoes (quieter)

**Reconnaissance Patrols**:
- **Wire cutters**: Cut through enemy wire
- **Compass**: Navigate in darkness (easy to get lost)
- **Watch**: Synchronize timing

**Raiding Patrols**:
- **Clubs**: Trench clubs (wooden, metal-studded; for silent killing)
- **Extra grenades**: 6-10 per man
- **Flare gun**: Signal success or call for help
- **Ropes**: Tie prisoners

**Wire Patrols**:
- **Barbed wire coils**: Heavy, awkward
- **Stakes**: Wooden or metal posts
- **Mallets**: Hammer stakes (loud; risky)
- **Pliers**: Cut and twist wire

**What They Did NOT Carry**:
- **Heavy equipment**: No backpacks, entrenching tools
- **Identification**: Removed ID discs sometimes (if captured, enemy couldn't identify unit)
- **Letters/photos**: Left behind (if killed, family items not lost in no-man's-land)

---

### **BRIEFING**

**What Officer Told Patrol** (15-30 minutes before departure):

1. **Mission**: What to do, where to go
2. **Route**: Path out and back (memorize; no maps carried usually)
3. **Timing**: Departure time, return time (late = assumed dead)
4. **Enemy**: Known German positions, sentries, patrols
5. **Password**: Challenge and response (returning patrol must give password or own sentries shoot)
6. **Signals**: Flares (red = trouble, green = success, white = retreat)
7. **Emergency Plan**: What to do if things go wrong

**Final Words**:
- "Move silently. Stay low. If you're seen, don't hesitate—kill or run. Be back by 03:00. Good luck."

---

## NIGHT PATROL: STEP-BY-STEP

### **PHASE 1: DEPARTURE (22:00-23:00)**

**Waiting in Trench**:
- Men huddle in trench, check equipment
- Smoke last cigarette (can't smoke in no-man's-land)
- Pray (some men; others just stare)
- Fear building (stomach churning, hands shaking)

**Climb Out**:
- Leader goes first
- One man at a time climbs parapet (slowly, silently)
- Lie flat on ground outside trench
- Wait for others
- Once all out: Begin crawl

**Sentries**:
- French sentries know patrol is out (warned beforehand)
- Still dangerous: Nervous sentry might shoot movement

**What You Feel**:
- Terror (leaving safety of trench)
- Exposure (sky above, nothing between you and enemy)
- Vulnerability (any moment could bring bullet, flare, shout)

---

### **PHASE 2: NO-MAN'S-LAND (23:00-01:00)**

**Terrain**:
- Churned mud, shell craters, barbed wire, corpses
- Uneven (trip hazards everywhere)
- Water-filled craters (fall in = drown or make noise)
- Smell: Death, rot, chemicals, mud

**Movement**:

**Crawling** (most common):
- Belly to ground
- Push with elbows/knees
- Painfully slow (100m takes 20-30 minutes)
- Clothing soaked in mud, water
- Wire tears skin, uniform

**Crouching** (if confident no observation):
- Low crouch, move slowly
- Faster than crawling (100m in 10 minutes)
- Riskier (silhouette visible if flare goes up)

**What You Hear**:
- Own breathing (sounds deafeningly loud to you)
- Rats (everywhere; scuttling over corpses, trash)
- Distant guns (constant background rumble)
- German voices (if near their trench; terrifying)
- Comrades behind you (rustling, breathing)

**What You See** (Very Little):
- Darkness (moon sometimes; clouds often)
- Shapes (craters, wire, corpses)
- Flares (suddenly illuminate everything)

**FLARES** (Constant Threat):

**What Happens**:
- Germans fire flares periodically (white light, burns 30-60 seconds, floats down on parachute)
- Lights up no-man's-land like daylight

**Survival**:
1. **FREEZE IMMEDIATELY** when flare goes up
   - Don't move a muscle
   - Don't look up (face reflects light)
   - Press into ground
2. **Pray**: Hope Germans don't see you
3. **Wait**: Flare burns out, darkness returns
4. **Resume**: Continue crawling

**If Seen**:
- Machine gun fire (sweeps area where movement spotted)
- Rifle fire (snipers target you)
- Grenades (if close to German trench)

**Result**:
- Dead: Killed instantly
- Wounded: Lie in no-man's-land, can't call for help (gives away position), bleed out or rescued hours later
- Escape: If lucky, crawl/run back to French trench

---

### **PHASE 3: OBJECTIVE (01:00-02:00)**

#### **RECONNAISSANCE PATROL**:

**Approach German Trench**:
- Crawl to within 20-50 meters
- Lie still, observe
- Listen (count voices, languages/accents, activity level)
- Watch (sentry positions, machine gun locations, wire gaps)
- Memorize (no notes; must remember)

**Duration**: 15-45 minutes (lie in mud, watch, listen)

**Danger**:
- German sentries walking parapet
- German patrols (might bump into each other in darkness)
- Own impatience (long time lying still, cold, fear building)

**Success**:
- Gather intel, withdraw undetected

---

#### **RAIDING PATROL**:

**Approach**:
- Cut wire (quietly; wire cutters, slow snips)
- Approach German trench (crawl last 10-20m)

**Attack**:
1. **Signal**: Leader throws grenade (starts attack)
2. **Charge**: Men rush forward, jump into German trench
3. **Kill**: Grenades into dugouts, shoot/stab Germans in trench
4. **Capture**: Grab 1-2 Germans, knock unconscious or threaten into submission
5. **Withdraw**: 5-10 minutes maximum, then run

**Combat**:
- **Close-quarters**: Pistol shots, bayonet stabs, grenade blasts
- **Chaos**: Darkness, smoke, screaming, confusion
- **Violence**: Brutal, fast, desperate

**Prisoner Handling**:
- Germans fight or surrender (varies)
- If resist: Kill
- If surrender: Drag back to French lines

**German Response**:
- Flares (illuminate area)
- Machine guns (sweep no-man's-land)
- Grenades (throw at raiders)
- Reinforcements (if raid lasts too long)

**Casualties**:
- High: 20-40% of raiding party killed or wounded

---

#### **WIRE REPAIR**:

**Work**:
- Unroll wire (heavy, tangles, catches on everything)
- Hammer stakes (LOUD; every blow echoes)
- Twist wire around stakes
- Test tension

**Danger**:
- Noise attracts German fire
- Germans sometimes wait, then ambush wire party
- Wire cuts hands (wearing gloves, but still)

**Interruption**:
- German machine gun opens fire (noise gave you away)
- Patrol scatters, crawls back to French trench
- Wire often left half-finished

---

#### **LISTENING POST**:

**Activity**:
- Lie in shell crater (2-3 men)
- Listen (ears strain for hours)
- Whisper reports to each other

**What You Hear** (If Germans Active):
- Digging (preparing trenches or tunnels)
- Talking (German voices; can't understand but note number)
- Equipment (clanking, wire cutting)
- Movement (footsteps, patrols)

**What You Do**:
- Remember everything
- Don't fall asleep (deadly; partner kicks you awake)
- Stay silent

**Duration**: Hours (2-6 hours sometimes)

**Danger**:
- German patrol stumbles upon you (fight or run)
- Artillery (shell lands in crater = death)
- Cold (hypothermia risk; can't move to stay warm)

---

### **PHASE 4: WITHDRAWAL (02:00-03:00)**

**Return**:
- Reverse route (crawl back toward French lines)
- Faster (urgency to reach safety)
- Still dangerous (Germans alert after raid, flares continue)

**Challenge**:
- Reach French wire (own barbed wire)
- Call out password: "Who goes there?"
- Sentry challenges: "Halt! Password!"
- Respond correctly or sentry shoots

**Password System**:
- Changed daily
- Example: Challenge = "Marseille!" / Response = "Lyon!"
- If wrong: Shot

**Injured**:
- Carry wounded (slow, exhausting)
- Sometimes leave dead (recover later)
- Wounded scream (give away position; sometimes gagged or told to be silent)

**Return to Trench**:
- Climb back into trench
- Collapse (exhaustion, relief)
- Report to officer

---

### **PHASE 5: DEBRIEF (03:00-04:00)**

**What Officer Asks**:
- What did you see?
- How many Germans?
- Defenses?
- Activity?

**Report**:
- Verbal (write report later)
- Details: Positions, numbers, equipment, mood (if heard talking)

**Prisoner** (If Captured):
- Handed to intelligence officers
- Interrogated (separate document: interrogation procedures)

**Casualties**:
- Count: Who's missing? Who's wounded?
- Recovery: Send party to retrieve dead/wounded (if possible)
- Burial: Dead buried later

**Reward**:
- Extra wine ration (sometimes)
- Rest (next day no duties, if possible)
- Nothing (often; just expected duty)

**What You Feel**:
- Relief (survived)
- Exhaustion (physical and emotional)
- Guilt (if comrades died)
- Dread (know you'll patrol again)

---

## WHAT COULD GO WRONG (AND DID)

### **1. LOST IN NO-MAN'S-LAND**

**How**:
- Darkness, disorientation
- Shell craters all look the same
- Compass broken or forgotten
- Panic

**Result**:
- Wander for hours
- Stumble into German trench (captured or killed)
- Die of exposure
- Found days later (corpse in no-man's-land)

**Prevention**:
- Memorize landmarks (dead tree, specific crater)
- Follow own wire back
- Stay calm

---

### **2. AMBUSH**

**How**:
- Germans knew patrol was coming (intelligence, pattern, noise)
- Germans waited silently
- Patrol walks into kill zone

**What Happens**:
- Germans open fire (close range, automatic)
- Grenades thrown
- Patrol annihilated or routed

**Survival**:
- Scatter immediately
- Don't bunch up
- Grenades back
- Run

**Casualties**:
- Often 50-100% (ambush deadly)

---

### **3. FRIENDLY FIRE**

**How**:
- Returning patrol gives wrong password (forgot, confused, wounded)
- Sentry nervous, shoots first
- Artillery shells no-man's-land randomly (hits own patrol)

**Result**:
- Killed by own side

**Frequency**:
- Not rare (tragic but happened regularly)

**Prevention**:
- Memorize password
- Announce return loudly ("Patrol returning! Don't shoot!")
- Coordinate with artillery (inform of patrol so they don't shell)

---

### **4. WIRE ENTANGLEMENT**

**How**:
- Barbed wire everywhere
- Crawling through wire
- Wire snags uniform, skin
- Struggle makes noise
- Stuck

**What Happens**:
- Germans hear struggling
- Flare goes up
- Machine gun fires
- Patrol member dies tangled in wire

**Survival**:
- Move slowly through wire (patience)
- Cut free (wire cutters)
- Don't panic

**Horror**:
- Wounded men tangled in wire, can't escape, die slowly

---

## SOLDIER ACCOUNTS: NIGHT PATROL TESTIMONIES

### **Account 1: Reconnaissance Patrol (Maurice Genevoix, 1916)**

> *"We crawled into the darkness. Every sound was a German. Every shadow was death. I lay in the mud for an hour, twenty meters from their trench, listening to them talk. I didn't understand German, but I counted eight voices. We crawled back. I was covered in mud and shaking. The officer asked what I saw. I could barely speak."*

---

### **Account 2: Raiding Party (Ernst Jünger, German perspective)**

> *"We heard them coming—Frenchmen, cutting our wire. We waited. When they entered our trench, we threw grenades. The blast lit up their faces for a moment—boys, terrified. We shot them. One ran. We let him go. He'd tell his comrades what happened here."*

---

### **Account 3: Wire Repair (Anonymous French soldier, diary)**

> *"Wire repair tonight. We carried the coils into no-man's-land. Every hammer blow sounded like thunder. The Germans fired flares. We froze. When the flare died, we hammered again. After an hour, a machine gun opened up. We dropped the wire and ran. Pierre didn't make it back."*

---

### **Account 4: Lost Patrol (Testimony, Norton Cru collection)**

> *"We got turned around. Couldn't find our trench. We wandered for three hours. At dawn, we were still out there. We hid in a crater all day, Germans fifty meters away. That night, we finally found our way back. The sentry almost shot us. We were reported dead."*

---

## GAME INTEGRATION

### **NIGHT PATROL GAMEPLAY MECHANICS**

#### **Stealth System**:
- **Visibility Meter**: Darkness (low), flares (high), moon (medium)
- **Noise Meter**: Crawling (low), walking (medium), running (high), wire/combat (maximum)
- **Detection**: Germans react to sight and sound
  - Spotted → Alert → Search → Attack

#### **Movement**:
- **Crawl**: Slow, silent, low profile (WASD + crouch, prone)
- **Crouch-Walk**: Medium speed, medium noise, medium profile
- **Run**: Fast, loud, high profile (emergency only)
- **Freeze**: On flare (button hold, character freezes mid-motion)

#### **Flare Mechanic**:
- **Random flares**: Germans fire periodically
- **Freeze immediately** (QTE: Press button within 0.5 seconds)
- **Hold still**: 30-60 seconds while flare burns
- **Movement detected**: Instant machine gun fire (death or severe damage)

#### **Wire Navigation**:
- **Wire obstacles**: Slow movement, damage if moving too fast
- **Cut wire**: Tool use (time investment, noise risk)
- **Entanglement**: Random chance if rushing (stuck, must free self)

#### **Objective Types** (Mission Variety):

1. **Recon**: Reach observation point, stay X minutes, return
2. **Raid**: Enter German trench, capture prisoner, escape
3. **Wire Repair**: Reach wire location, repair (progress bar), return
4. **Listening Post**: Stay in crater, listen for audio cues (German activity), return

#### **Patrol Composition** (Squad AI):
- **Leader** (Player or NPC): Makes decisions
- **Squadmates**: 2-5 NPCs
  - Follow player
  - React to threats
  - Can be killed (permanent loss)

#### **Password System**:
- **Return to trench**: Must type/speak password correctly
- **Wrong password**: Warning shot, then lethal fire
- **No password**: Assume German infiltrator, shoot on sight

#### **Fear/Stress System**:
- **Stress Meter**: Increases in no-man's-land
  - Flares increase stress
  - Combat increases stress
  - Time in no-man's-land increases stress
- **High stress effects**:
  - Shaky aim
  - Heavy breathing (audible)
  - Tunnel vision
  - Increased mistakes

#### **Permadeath Option** (Hardcore Mode):
- Die on patrol = permanent death
- Save only on successful return

#### **Rewards**:
- **Intel**: Unlock enemy position information (map markers)
- **Prisoners**: Cutscene interrogation, story progression
- **Survival**: Extra rations, rest day (buff for next mission)

---

## HISTORICAL NOTES

**Authenticity**: ✅ All patrol types, procedures, and dangers documented from manuals and soldier accounts.

**Sources**:
- French Army: *Manuel du Chef de Section d'Infanterie* (1916, patrol tactics)
- Jünger, Ernst: *Storm of Steel* (1920, German perspective on patrols)
- Genevoix, Maurice: *Ceux de 14* (1916)
- Pézard, André: *Nous autres à Vauquois* (1918)
- British accounts: Sassoon, Graves (similar patrol procedures, Western Front)
- After-action reports (Archives de la Guerre, Vincennes)

---

**END NIGHT PATROL DOCUMENT**
